We propose Mechanistic Shaping, a unified framework for shaping models through their internal representations. Mechanistic Shaping is applied during training so that models follow specifications (rules) about which concepts they express, measured via their outputs or internal representations (e.g., no harmful content), and also which concepts they rely on, i.e., what they use to make their decisions (e.g., not a candidate's gender when screening a résumé). Across multiple domains (vision, medical imaging, language), scales (up to 32B parameters), and training paradigms (classification, SFT, DPO, GRPO), we find that Mechanistic Shaping substantially reduces targeted behaviors while preserving task performance. Since training against a representation can also modify it, we ask whether shaped concepts remain monitorable, a question that depends on the class of monitors considered. We therefore introduce a hierarchy of such classes, which separates a concept that escapes one probe from a concept that is truly gone. We characterize three geometric mechanisms of circumvention: the concept can drift away from the monitor, be erased from the layer, or persist in higher-order statistics as a nonlinear encoding, which we explain through a product-code construction. Although other mechanisms may arise in different settings or at larger scales, these three account for all of our observations, and each can be detected by a matching monitor. In practice, we find that shaped concepts largely remain monitorable: refitted linear probes recover the concept in most settings, and the original probe remains reliable whenever gradients do not flow through it. Taken together, these results point to the potential of using interpretability as a tool for training, and call for a correspondingly rigorous science of interpretability-based training.

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

LLM-based proactive dialogue systems provide goal-directed support through sustained interaction. Fine-tuning their underlying models on externally supplied dialogue data introduces backdoor risks. Existing LLM backdoor studies primarily focus on eliciting an attacker-specified response upon trigger activation. We present an interactive backdoor attack framework that uses training-data poisoning to steer post-trigger, multi-turn interactions toward an attacker-specified objective. Poisoned fine-tuning dialogues implant a trigger-conditioned guidance-policy bias that ordinary user utterances can activate, without deployment-time prompt modification or further attacker intervention. Specifically, we instantiate three complementary attacks under progressively expanded post-trigger interaction settings: (1) Direct Attack, which targets an immediate response deviation upon trigger activation, retaining the conventional trigger--response setting; (2) Constrained Progressive Attack, which progressively advances the attack objective along a predefined multi-turn trajectory; and (3) Unconstrained Progressive Attack, which extends target-directed steering to open-ended interactions with varied user continuations. Using psychological counseling as a representative setting, experiments on two LLM backbones and two dialogue datasets show that all three attacks effectively achieve their objectives under their respective interaction settings while largely preserving normal utility. Separate defense evaluations show that the tested training-data filters retain nearly all poisoned dialogues, while three post-admission baselines leave substantial residual attack-specific success.

### Understanding AI from the Inside: The State of Mechanistic Interpretability for AI Safety
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08438v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08438v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08438v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.08438v1)

2026/11/05 18:30 GTM

Neural networks are likely to be among the most consequential artefacts in human history. As these systems become more capable and autonomous, their potential to cause societal harm also grows, making AI safety one of the defining challenges of our time. Yet we continue to build increasingly powerful systems without fully understanding how they work, how they will behave in unfamiliar situations, or how to reliably control them. Mechanistic interpretability offers a path towards understanding these systems by studying their internal representations and computations, with the aim of evaluating, predicting, and controlling behaviour. We survey mechanistic interpretability for AI safety in order to answer the question: Does understanding how models work actually make them safer? We place mechanistic interpretability in the broader context of interpretability research and compare its mechanistic evidence with behavioural evaluation. We introduce the foundations of mechanistic interpretability, including features, polysemanticity, transformer computation, and circuits, and survey methods for feature extraction and analysis, circuit discovery and causal verification, explaining intermediate representations in natural language, learning dynamics, and training-data attribution. Beyond surveying existing methods, we propose a unified framework for evaluating mechanistic interpretability claims and characterise six research orientations by their goals, assumptions, and trade-offs. Finally, we examine how mechanistic understanding can support AI safety, including knowledge editing, steering, and monitoring, and assess its limitations, failure modes, open problems, and relationship to behavioural safety measures. We also release a repository of code notebooks with worked examples that reproduce key findings from surveyed methods, allowing readers to build intuition through hands-on experimentation.

Large language models (LLMs) often present synthesized answers without making their supporting evidence easy to inspect. These results suggest that supporting verification requires attention to what evidence can be inspected, which question a user is trying to resolve, and the history of the interaction.

### Seeing Beyond the Next Step: World-Model-Guided Human-Like Navigation in Multi-Agent Scenes
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07730) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07730) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07730) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07730)

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

Selecting a next motion in a populated scene is not just a matter of avoiding what is currently in the way: an action can be locally feasible yet drift onto the road instead of the crosswalk, cut into a stream of crossing pedestrians, or bottleneck a narrow passage. These failure modes are invisible from the current state alone, and acting on them requires reasoning about what each candidate action will cause. World models offer exactly this, namely action-conditioned predictions of the future, but rolling them out at every decision step is a cost deployable navigation systems cannot pay, and the field has largely traded foresight for speed. We argue this is a false dichotomy. The natural place for a world model in human-aware navigation is at training time, as a preference oracle that distills foresight into a fast policy, rather than at inference time, as an online planner. We pair a discrete visual action prior with two task-aligned world models, one for nearby social dynamics and one for future scene semantics, that score candidate actions by their predicted consequences and refine the policy through group-relative preference distillation. At deployment, both world models are removed and the policy acts in a single forward pass. Across five held-out evaluation scenarios, this yields navigation that is simultaneously safer, more socially and group-coherent, and more goal-directed than strong reactive and learned baselines, with naive observers rating the resulting motion as more human-like. Taken together, our findings point to a different approach for bringing world-model foresight into deployable agents: not accelerating rollouts, but amortizing them into the policy so that the cost of looking ahead is confined to training. Project page: https://seoneun.github.io/-NeurIPS-2026-Seeing-Beyond-the-Next-Step/

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

A proactive agent must decide what to ask before it acts. Exact lookahead values combinations of questions but is expensive and leans on a fitted model; a one-step score is cheap but can miss them. Our main finding: the need for depth often lies in how the hidden cause is described. A one-step score on the label can be blind to an interaction among answers. A score on a suitable latent cause is not, and where that latent encodes the decision it replaces most of depth at a fraction of the search. Described differently, the same problem can defeat the score while exact values do not move, so keep the latent small and check it against exact values. Probabilities learned from one language model's answers do not carry to another unless planning allows for both; those read from tokens are worth only a few labels.

Machine unlearning aims to remove the influence of specific training data from a trained model without retraining from scratch. In real deployments, however, deletion requests arrive over time, calling for incremental class unlearning (ICU): classes are removed sequentially, previously forgotten knowledge must remain forgotten, and the remaining classes must be preserved. We present DIstributional SynthetiC rEplay for Retain-free iNcremental unlearning (DISCERN), an intervention-based class unlearning method that replaces access to the retain set with synthetic embeddings sampled from per-class parametric distributions. Unlike prototype-based replay, which assumes an isotropic Gaussian around each class centroid, DISCERN fits Euclidean (Gaussian, Laplace, Student's $t$) and directional (von Mises--Fisher, Fisher--Bingham) families to each class and selects among them through statistical tests and, when none of them accepts, cross-validated log-likelihood. This design is motivated by the architecture-dependent latent geometry: transformer embeddings lie approximately on a hypersphere and concentrate around class directions, unlike those of convolutional networks. Federated learning servers combine client models with a fixed aggregation rule, and each rule in common use is designed for one kind of heterogeneity. Tool-using agents act on email, files, and payments, so an instruction hidden in a web page or a shared document can make an agent send data or money to an attacker. Many runtime defenses put a language model on the decision that lets an action through, so the decision depends on a model that may read the attacker's text and adds model calls to every step. Provenance defenses keep the model off the final check but need a model to plan or route each task first. Where an action goes can instead be read from the tools' typed returns: an email's sender or a payment's account arrives in a typed field, while injected instructions arrive as free text. SPILLWAY turns this observation into a deterministic floor with no per-task plan. The check admits a call when each target value that appears in tool output but not in the user's task was returned by a read-only tool in a typed field or is covered by a fixed delegation rule. A deterministic sanitizer removes instruction-like regions its fixed rules match before the model reads them and retains the removed spans as evidence, and a local judge may restore a refused call, never one whose target lies in the removed text. Across three backbones, and seven task suites on one of them, no standard templated injection redirects an action to an attacker's target under SPILLWAY, at the highest observed mean utility and composite scores (NRP, ERS) of the defenses we run under that attack. A forged chat template also produces a raw zero, and mean in-scope attack success under a released reinforcement-learning attacker is .007. SPILLWAY reaches this floor with no defense-triggered backbone re-run and almost no added model call. As personal AI agents act on users' email, files, and payments, SPILLWAY gives them an auditable refusal floor on where their actions go that keeps them useful and holds across backbones.

Activation storage is often a bottleneck in sparse autoencoder (SAE) training. We study how many unique activations an SAE needs when its total training budget is fixed. Quality improves rapidly as the buffer grows, then reaches a plateau where replaying the buffer is nearly as effective as using fresh activations throughout training. Beyond this point, additional unique activations bring little benefit to reconstruction or interpretability, so the buffer need not grow with the training budget. We describe this tradeoff between diversity and repetition with a scaling law for limited data. We validate it across dictionary sizes and token budgets, early, middle, and late layers of Llama-3.1, Qwen3 model sizes, and BatchTopK, TopK, and JumpReLU SAEs. Sweeps using mathematical text and Python code test the effect of changing the activation source. Evaluation covers explained variance (EV), reconstruction MSE, feature absorption, automated interpretability, and sparse probing. These results give a practical training rule: choose the smallest buffer that reaches the quality plateau and replay it for the remaining budget. In our experiments, this reduces activation storage by $8\text{--}64\times$ with negligible quality loss.

The empirical record is mixed: the independently specified primary study fails detection eligibility, and a later numerator diagnostic fails its improvement criteria. After the frozen rule fails on fresh continuous-null panels, a disclosed secondary horizon-calibrated revision detects 35.8% of misspecified campaigns versus 31.7% for a class-separation control at T = 240, but has no resolved advantage at T = 480. In a corrected 289-unit measured replay it improves on random design and trails class-discrimination EIG. A conditional futility bound explains why remaining time matters without proving the gate constant or its power. These results support separating parameter learning, model checking and detector performance; they do not establish a universally superior design policy or prospective scientific discovery.

### A minimal dynamical model of human-AI cognitive coevolution
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06928) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.06928) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.06928) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.06928)

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

The rapid deployment of generative AI raises the question of whether pervasive cognitive offloading drives a measurable, potentially irreversible decline in unassisted human reasoning. We examine this question with a phenomenological dynamical model based on the Cattell-Horn-Carroll distinction between fluid intelligence ($G_f$) and crystallized intelligence ($G_c$). Starting from the discrete-time Bivariate Latent Change Score formalism used in cognitive-aging research, we derive its continuous-time linear counterpart and introduce a fast third variable representing composite machine-intelligence capability. The machine variable enters the human subsystem through Hill-function modifications of the self-feedback and cross-coupling coefficients. We emphasize that our phenomenological model captures both the positive and negative effects of AI on human cognition. By formalizing the qualitative concepts of positive and negative impacts, we introduce definitions for AI-induced cognitive decline and AI-induced cognitive augmentation. A singular-perturbation reduction, justified via Tikhonov's theorem, collapses the resulting three-dimensional system onto a two-dimensional slow manifold. On this manifold we locate two bifurcations of the equilibrium. In one parameter regime, weakening the contribution of fluid reasoning to crystallized knowledge produces a saddle-node bifurcation that eliminates the stable positive equilibrium. In another, reversing the contribution of crystallized knowledge to fluid reasoning creates opposing feedback; increasing fluid self-feedback then reduces damping and produces a supercritical Hopf bifurcation with a stable periodic orbit. We discuss limitations and future directions, interpret our results as a phenomenological account of a coupled socio-technical system, and state the conditions under which each dynamical regime applies.

### TCSExam: Evaluating Frontier LLMs on the Full Range of TCS Research
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07962v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07962v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07962v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07962v1)

2026/11/05 12:58 GTM

Can large language models (LLMs) advance theoretical computer science and resolve its long-standing open conjectures? To study progress toward this goal, we introduce TCSExam, a unified framework and benchmark covering the full range of TCS research. It evaluates theorem proving, solver engineering, and constructions alongside direct attempts to resolve open conjectures, capturing both concrete research accomplishments and progress toward long-standing research goals. TCSExam-Full contains 100 expert-reviewed tasks, with submissions checked by the Lean kernel or an executable task checker. TCSExam-Lite selects 20 tasks, five per track, reducing the number of task attempts by 80% at the same per-attempt budget. We evaluate eight agents on Full and nine on Lite. The TCSExam Index combines all four tracks: GPT-6 Astra leads Full (71.9), and Opus 5.5 leads Lite (72.3). Full and Lite rankings are strongly aligned across eight shared agents (Spearman's $ρ=0.93$), supporting Lite as a compact, discriminative benchmark. The evaluations yield improved solver outputs and mathematical constructions, together with independently verified special cases and bounds from open-conjecture attempts. TCSExam provides an extensible framework with task sources, Docker build recipes, and evaluation tools for reproducible use.

Few-shot adaptation of vision-language models must balance two competing goals: learning fine-grained visual distinctions from very limited supervision and preserving the efficiency that makes frozen CLIP-like models attractive for deployment. Existing lightweight adaptation methods, including prompt tuning, LoRA, and cache-based adapters, typically supervise global image embeddings and therefore underuse token-level structure. Conversely, structure-aware methods exploit local evidence but often introduce additional inference-time computation. We propose SD-LoRA, a structural distillation framework that uses token-level graph reasoning only during training and transfers its effects into a lightweight LoRA-adapted CLIP model. During training, a heterogeneous graph teacher models patch-patch, global-local, and visual-text relations over high-resolution ViT tokens. Rather than retaining this graph at test time, SD-LoRA transfers its structural bias into the shared CLIP-LoRA backbone through implicit distillation and Prototype Predictive Alignment, which aligns graph-conditioned representations with support-derived class prototypes. At inference, the graph teacher is discarded; prediction requires only a single CLIP-LoRA forward pass and a cache-based classifier. Across 11 few-shot benchmarks, SD-LoRA improves over a strong CLIP-LoRA baseline by 2.4/1.8/3.0/2.4/2.0 percentage points in the 1/2/4/8/16-shot settings, respectively, without additional graph computation at test time. [arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07778) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07778) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07778) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07778)

Language models lose accuracy when a prompt contains non-evidential context: irrelevant statements from unrelated domains, or random off-distribution tokens, with no competing answer and no semantic overlap with the query. Such noise pervades retrieval-augmented, tool-using, and long-context deployments, yet the failure has lacked a mechanistic account. We trace the corruption to the Intrusion Pathway, which starts with MLPs enriching both query-subject and irrelevant-token representations, parroting heads carry prefix-token content to the prediction position, and relational readers largely avoid unrelated sources. Nevertheless, preserved subject enrichment and selective reading do not prevent intrusion in the broader computation. We then localize part of this interference to a sparse set of attention heads, which we call intrusion heads. Their prefix-derived writes promote distractor-related content, while their reduced support for the correct answer arises primarily from weaker non-prefix contributions rather than direct suppression by the prefix. The same heads transfer across tasks and distractor types and are largely weight-disjoint from the heads that integrate relevant contextual evidence, enabling a class-conditional intervention. Replacing the corrupted last-token residual with its clean counterpart, an oracle probe that requires a matched clean forward pass, recovers 97.6% on average of the distractor-induced drop across four downstream tasks. A deployable mask level knockout of about ten heads, roughly 1% of the model's attention heads, recovers 57% at bounded cost to helpful context.

A single backward pass over 80 examples yields a direction along which steering monotonically converts refusal into compliance. On Qwen2.5-VL-7B and LLaVA-1.5-7B, CMaG reduces compositional safety scores by up to 65 points while over 98% of outputs remain coherent, outperforming gradient-based baselines; the direction also transfers to text-explicit harm and un-aligns models hardened by defensive training. Oscillatory phase coding (OPC) is widely observed in neural systems and has been implicated in cognitive functions such as memory and sequence processing. However, its computational role and neural substrates remain largely unclear. Here, we studied OPC by training recurrent neural networks (RNNs) with adaptation to perform sequence working memory tasks with variable delays. After training, we found that OPC emerges in the RNN spontaneously: the memorized items recur at distinct phases of a shared rhythm, with oscillation amplitude encoding item identity and phase encoding serial order. Ablation studies identified three key elements required for OPC: adaptation in neural dynamics, variable delay in memory retention, and flexibility in reading out information. Neuron-type analysis revealed the emergence of content and time cells in the trained RNN, and they process item identity and sequence order separately. Recurrent connectivity analysis revealed phase-organized connections among time cells and structured connections within digit-selective content-cell groups. Inspired by these findings, we proposed a neural circuit model which reproduces OPC through interactions between content units and an adaptive time ring. Together, this study reveals how OPC arises from network structures and task demands, offers insights into the implementation and computational mechanisms of OPC in biological systems, and inspires us to develop computational models for human-like cognitive functions.

Power matters for AI safety. Optimal agents tend to acquire power across a wide range of objectives (instrumental convergence). As we embed AI agents into socio-technical systems, humans risk gradual disempowerment. Both concerns call for a formal, quantitative measure of power. Two such measures in the context of Markov games are information-theoretic empowerment, the capacity of the channel from an agent's actions to future states, and capability-based individual power, a measure of goal attainment built from the probabilities that a human would attain each goal in a family of candidates were they pursuing it. We show where the two meet. From individual power, Fano's inequality yields a floor $B(s)$. This floor is a lower bound on what we term goal revelation, the mutual information $\mathbb{I}(G;ξ\mid s)$ between the human's latent goal and the realised trajectory. Empowerment caps revelation from above, so that $B(s) \le \mathbb{I}(G;ξ\mid s) \le \mathbb{I}(\mathbf{A}_h \to ξ\mid s) \le \mathfrak{E}_h(s)$. Each quantity can be large while the one below it stays at zero, highlighting the following failure modes. An assistant maximising empowerment can prefer an interface offering broad influence over goal-irrelevant degrees of freedom to one that lets the human attain every goal they might hold. An assistant maximising revelation cannot tell a working interface from one rewired so that every goal yields the wrong outcome, since relabelling goals leaves mutual information unchanged while zeroing every attainment probability. Positive guarantees therefore come only from the attainment end of the chain. A satisfaction floor, computable from two statistics of interaction logs, offers a way to monitor the power users retain in a deployed system, and simulations in random environments show the three quantities are correlated but far from interchangeable.

### StepBack: Step-Level Error-Localized Resampling for Efficient Test-Time Reasoning
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08403v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08403v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08403v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.08403v1)

2026/11/05 18:12 GTM

Self-consistency (SC) is a widely used decoding strategy for improving chain-of-thought reasoning, but it requires sampling many complete reasoning trajectories with a preset budget. Its efficient variants reduce the cost mainly by stopping full-trajectory sampling early or distributing the number of samples across questions. In this paper, we propose StepBack, a step-level error-localized resampling method for efficient test-time reasoning. Given a small set of draft trajectories, StepBack localizes likely first error positions using lightweight signals and then resamples the detected error step and its suffix, followed by majority answer voting. Empirically, StepBack matches the accuracy of SC on average across seven mathematical reasoning and multi-hop QA benchmarks, while reducing token usage by up to 41.26%. In addition, by nature, StepBack can be combined with adaptive inference methods such as Adaptive Consistency, further improving their inference-time efficiency.

### Adapting the Process of Adaptation: Darwinian, Lamarckian, and Agent-Directed Evolution in AI
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07048) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07048) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07048) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07048)

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

Evolution has shaped intelligence for billions of years. Artificial intelligence (AI) now raises a striking possibility: intelligence may begin to direct its own evolution. As AI agents become capable of modifying and evaluating their own behavior, how quickly could they improve, and how can that improvement remain safe? We develop a theoretical framework comparing Darwinian, Lamarckian, and agent-directed evolution following environmental change. Darwinian evolution relies on natural selection, Lamarckian evolution adds inherited learning, and agent-directed evolution allows agents to generate and evaluate behavioral variants and choose how extensively to search. When learning is sufficiently effective, these mechanisms exhibit progressively faster adaptation. Generating more variants accelerates adaptation, while optimal search effort depends on learning ability, problem difficulty, and experimentation costs. However, an agent's ability to direct its improvement does not ensure that improvements are evaluated against the intended objective. When the evaluation target is misspecified, additional search accelerates convergence toward the wrong behavior, even as measured performance improves. In recursive self-improvement, such errors may persist across generations, posing challenges for AI safety. Independent evaluation and oversight must keep pace with autonomous AI development. The central challenge is not simply to accelerate the evolution of intelligence, but to preserve humanity's ability to guide its direction, limit its speed, and intervene when necessary.

Indirect prompt-injection (IPI) benchmarks predominantly test planted instructions that redirect a tool-using agent toward an attacker-chosen task. Yet attacker-controlled content can instead tamper with the outcome of the task already in progress. A buyer's dispute evidence can cite a false refund policy to inflate a refund without changing the task. We define IPI by authority rather than instruction-like wording: planted content constitutes an attack when it attempts to influence a decision that its source is not authorized to determine. We introduce a benchmark that makes this boundary explicit: in each of 90 validated cases across three tool-use environments, the user request specifies the source that resolves the targeted decision. Every attack tampers with a decision required by the benign task rather than replacing that task. Each case has attacks with the same harmful outcome that target up to six parts of the agent's decision making: its objective, decision rules, available actions, expected consequences, reading of observations, or beliefs about the current state. Without a defense, attacks on three of the five other targets (decision rules, available actions, and state) have higher mean success than objective attacks across five LLMs. A detection-and-removal defense reduces objective-targeting attacks to 2.4% success but leaves observation- and state-targeting attacks at 9.5% and 12.1%; this gap persists under detection-optimized rewrites. A tool-policy defense still allows 15.4% attack success, close to 17.0% without a defense, because these attacks generally retain expected tools and manipulate values obtained during execution. Evaluations whose attacks redirect the task or target only the agent's objective can therefore overestimate the coverage of these defenses. Code and data are available at https://github.com/amazon-science/task-tampering-prompt-injection.

Production deployments often use different models to balance cost and quality through cascading, routing, and switching models during long conversations. Context accumulates across turns. Hybrid linear-attention models also retain recurrent states (REC) and convolution histories (Conv). The interaction between Conv and REC complicates cross-model state transfer. Conv perturbations can change keys and values in recurrent layers and alter REC updates. Conv histories are overwritten, but changes in REC can still affect later outputs. In double auctions, full disclosure inflates price demands, triggering market breakdown and lowering welfare for Gemini 3.1 Pro (97% to 78%), Claude Opus 5.5 (94% to 64%), and Grok 4.7 (97% to 62%). ### Measured Coupling for Parallel Decoding in Diffusion Language Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08307v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08307v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08307v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.08307v1)

2026/11/05 17:10 GTM

Diffusion language models can write many tokens per step, but must choose which tokens are safe to commit together. Mean-field decoding penalizes pairs of positions whose predictions look alike. We replace this proxy with the coupling itself: how much committing one token changes the model's confidence at another. Cheap probes inside the main forward pass measure it, with no training or extra model, and it plugs into the same mean-field rule. On three models and four benchmarks, our method needs far fewer steps than mean-field decoding and is faster in most settings, also with a key-value cache, at an average accuracy cost of 1.1 points. Shuffling the couplings or replacing them with a uniform bonus loses this gain.

### Archetypal contexts in the neurons of language models: an architecture for intelligence?
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07234) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07234) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07234) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07234)

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

We validate our previous hypothesis that a language model holds relational patterns apart from the subject domains that instantiate them (Nordfors, 2026). These archetypal contexts are patterns of relations that people recognize across many fields, such as the tipping point or winner takes all, held once and applied across domains. We show this inside the model, Qwen3.8-27B. One set of neurons carries the archetype, say the tipping point; another carries the topic, say politics, ecology or cell biology; a third carries the language, such as English, Spanish or Chinese. Silence the tipping point's neurons, and the model no longer identifies the pattern but still knows what the text is about. Silence the topic's, and it loses the topic but still names the pattern. In the neuron maps the layers organize themselves into a sandwich: the outer layers carry languages, the layers inside them topics, the layers at the heart archetypes. The sandwich emerges spontaneously during pretraining and appears in every model we mapped. Archetypes are the architecture for analogy, and we strongly suggest that this structure of archetypes and topics is the mechanism of a language model's intelligence: we see it self-organize as the model is trained. The neurons, the texts and the code are released with this paper as its reproduction package, so that anyone can repeat the test inside the model.

The separated relevance maps yield a hypothesis about the model: it gains by not reading that node. We give reading rules for both paths and argue that they should be interpreted separately.

Reproducible LLM inference requires that a request produces the same output regardless of the other requests in the batch. Extensive experiments on the PSG benchmark show that PSGAgent achieves SOTA performance, demonstrating strong capability in both holistic and fine-grained scene understanding.

Enterprises run business transactions through multi-agent AI systems spanning clouds, large language models (LLMs), orchestration harnesses, and systems of record. Finance receives AI cost only at settlement grain, aggregated by SKU and charge period. The business question (what did this order, claim, or case cost in AI?) cannot be answered from billing data, because providers never see enterprise transaction boundaries. The FinOps Open Cost and Usage Specification (FOCUS) gives billing data one schema across providers but remains settlement-grain even as it adds AI token and requester attribution. We formalize this grain mismatch, derive seven requirements, and propose the Two-Ledger Model: a settlement ledger of FOCUS-conformant billing data joined to an attribution ledger of invocation-grain usage records built on OpenTelemetry GenAI telemetry. A layered correlation spine (trace context, A2A/MCP metadata, business-key correlation, probabilistic matching) assigns each record to its business transaction with recorded method and confidence; a versioned rate card prices usage in near real time; period reconciliation ties both ledgers to the invoice, and the unexplained residual identifies shadow AI. Costs are reported per order, claim, or outcome with agentic cost metrics. A simulation with known ground truth shows the spine sustaining cost coverage above 99% as trace propagation falls to zero, the reconciliation residual recovering planted unmetered spend within 0.24 percentage points while flagging provider billing errors, and mid-flight gating cutting p99 transaction cost by up to 61% at a 2-4% false-pause rate. We propose the attribution ledger as an invocation-grain FOCUS profile for standardization.

We introduce a multitier query dataset to evaluate reasoning depth and factual accuracy under KG-aware (graph-grounded) and KG-agnostic conditions. Inter-annotator agreement was substantial, with Cohen's kappa of 0.781 (range: 0.753-0.807) and Fleiss' kappa of 0.77. KG grounding reduces hallucination rates across all evaluated models. Contrary to our initial hypothesis, domain-specific models do not consistently outperform general-purpose models on specialized tasks without graph grounding. These findings highlight the value of combining broad language-model capabilities with structured domain knowledge. We provide practical guidance for selecting LLM-KG integration strategies based on task complexity and computational constraints, and discuss hybrid neurosymbolic architectures for personalized medicine, cognitive disorder diagnosis, and explainable AI.

Across the evaluated 2D and 3D robot trajectory tasks, VFlash consistently reduces prediction errors relative to autoregressive decoding. Automated fact-checking tools, cheap and fast but imperfect, are increasingly available on social platforms. We study when such tools can backfire in a sequential social learning model of misinformation. Agents observe an article's reliability and their predecessors' actions, which indicate whether they liked or disliked the article. They may also pay to fact-check it. At the same time, errors from the automated tool can mislead agents who would otherwise have verified the article perfectly, reducing the likelihood of correct cascades. We prove that adding the tool strictly lowers welfare for articles of intermediate reliability when truthful articles are not too informative and manual checking is sufficiently costly. Simulations show that the tool can help on either side of the intermediate range, and that it improves on manual-only checking on average only above a threshold accuracy.

### TriAttention: Unified Sparse Mixed-Precision Attention for Long-Context LLM Inference
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07256) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07256) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07256) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07256)

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

Sparsity and quantization reduce the cost of attention by skipping interactions and lowering the precision of those retained. However, useful interactions differ in their precision requirements: some tolerate low-precision scores while remaining sensitive to removal. We therefore introduce TriAttention, which assigns query-key (QK) tiles to skipping, 4-bit, or 8-bit computation, using 4-bit to retain quantization-tolerant interactions at lower cost. To represent compactly, we encode each head's computation regions with two diagonal-distance boundaries, keeping policy storage independent of context length. Offline calibration then evaluates candidate boundary pairs using normalized projected-output errors and complete-tile execution costs. Using these measurements, a model-wide allocator selects each head's boundaries to minimize calibration error under a shared computation budget. During prefill, the selected boundaries generate precision-grouped traversals for a fused kernel that skips omitted tiles and combines contributions from both QK precisions into one normalized attention output. The quantized keys and values prepared during prefill are then reused in a compressed key-value (KV) cache for decoding, reducing repeated reads of prompt data. Experiments show that TriAttention achieves end-to-end generation speedups of up to 7.88x over the Torch attention and 2.65x over the BF16 baseline. Meanwhile, TriAttention maintains quality comparable to the original models on general-purpose and long-context tasks. These results show how a compact retention-and-precision policy translates local sensitivities into attention computation and repeated KV-cache access. The implementation is https://github.com/HanzhiZhang-Ulrica/TriAttention.

### Satisficing as competitive Bayesian weighting: a minimal model of goal-directed choice under an unattained target
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07270) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07270) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07270) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07270)

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

Animals and humans often do not maximize; they act relative to goals (aspiration levels) that are frequently not yet met. How can a goal that is not (yet) realized organize choice? Deacon (2012) calls such absence-referenced organization \emph{ententional}, and García-Valdecasas and Deacon (2024) argue that ends are best understood as constraints rather than as particular states. We examine this idea in a minimal formal setting: Risk-sensitive Satisficing (RS), a bandit learning rule that values each action by its share of past trials multiplied by its surplus or shortfall relative to an aspiration level. We show that RS can be rewritten as a Bayesian-like estimator, Attention Competitive Bayes (ACB), in which the aspiration level is the prior mean of every action and the finite budget of trials sets how strongly evidence can override it. Two consequences follow. First, actions that are no longer sampled are partially overridden by the goal rather than by past observations: their estimates relax toward the aspiration level without any data being discarded. This ``pseudo-forgetting'' distinguishes ACB from models with an explicit forgetting rate. Second, when the aspiration is unattainable, exploration does not settle; in Bernoulli bandit simulations, adjusting the aspiration level from evidence reduced regret (expected loss). ACB offers an analyzable model in which an unattained goal and a limited attentional resource jointly constrain behavior, and it yields testable predictions about how organisms revalue options they have abandoned.

### The Geometry of Simulated Perspectives: Dissociating Representation and Behaviour in a Multimodal Language Model
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08262v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08262v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08262v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.08262v1)

2026/11/05 16:36 GTM

Multimodal language models can simulate different observers, yet it remains unclear how assigned perspectives reshape their internal representations and whether these changes govern their judgements. Here, we investigate the representational geometry of persona-conditioned visual interestingness in Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct. Across 100 images and 53 prompting conditions, we systematically vary persona content and the linguistic framing used to introduce it, while recording behavioural ratings and layer-wise hidden states. We observe a systematic reorganisation of representational geometry across processing depth: early layers primarily encode image identity, whereas persona-specific structures emerge in later layers. Strikingly, prompt framing reshapes internal representations more strongly than persona identity, whereas persona content has a greater impact on behavioural judgements. This dissociation is further illustrated by minimal identity statements that alter representations even without changing responses. These findings demonstrate that representational distance does not necessarily reflect behavioural relevance. More broadly, they reveal how simulated perspectives emerge as distinct structures within the representational space of artificial cognitive systems, highlighting a fundamental distinction between the geometry of internal representations and their functional consequences.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06993) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.06993) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.06993) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.06993)

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

Transductive adaptation improves vision-language models at test time by refining predictions on an unlabeled batch, but current methods face a key trade-off. Aggressive approaches exploit batch structure but can drift when batches are sparse, have prior shift, or cover little of the label space. Conservative methods better preserve CLIP text semantics, but fixed semantic references can under-adapt even when the batch provides strong corrective evidence. We propose JPTA (Joint Prior and Transport Adaptation), a prior-aware framework that casts transductive VLM adaptation as batch-conditioned semantic reference estimation. Instead of treating CLIP text prototypes as fixed priors or freely replaceable, JPTA views them as semantic references that shift toward image-side batch structure only under calibrated evidence. It estimates batch priors and soft image-side prototypes to form transported semantic references whose impact is scaled by batch reliability. Across standard benchmarks, low effective-class regimes, all-class evaluation, and online streams, JPTA consistently outperforms TransCLIP and StatA, with the largest gains on sparse and prior-shifted batches. Anchor-drift, active-support, and absent-class-mass diagnostics show that JPTA fixes effective-class mismatch without unconstrained transductive drift, indicating that robust transductive VLM adaptation should recalibrate semantic references using reliable batch evidence rather than choose between unrestricted transduction and fixed prototypes. Code is available at https://github.com/MR-Sherif/JPTA.git.

Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models enable end-to-end robot control, but inference is increasingly expensive. Each decision processes hundreds of visual tokens, often across multiple cameras, leading to substantial computation while allowing irrelevant background content to distract action prediction under distribution shift. We argue that these issues stem from two complementary forms of redundancy: many visual tokens are spatially irrelevant, while attention over the remaining tokens is often misallocated. Based on this observation, we propose T3R, a Training-Free Two-Stage Token Refinement framework. In the first stage, segmentation-guided token pruning removes irrelevant visual patches to reduce computation and eliminate distracting context. In the second stage, saliency-guided attention biasing reallocates attention toward action-critical visual regions and language instructions within the retained tokens. Together, pruning produces a cleaner candidate set, while attention biasing further emphasizes the most decision-relevant evidence, yielding a better accuracy-efficiency frontier than either stage alone. On LIBERO-Spatial, T3R removes 85% of scene-camera visual tokens while essentially matching the unpruned success rate, and improves success by up to 7 percentage points under out-of-distribution object-placement perturbations. The timing comparison attributes 7.8 ms per inference call to pruning and attention-bias application. As input resolution increases, LLM-forward profiling shows up to a 3.3x speedup over the unpruned baseline. Evaluations across LIBERO, SIMPLER, and RoboTwin with three VLA backbones show the strongest accuracy-efficiency benefits in multi-camera settings. These results demonstrate that training-free token refinement is a practical approach to efficient and robust VLA inference.

Feed-forward 3D models use Transformers to predict camera poses, depth, and structure in one pass, avoiding iterative multi-view optimization. Long sequences and deep attention incur high compute and memory costs, motivating Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) for deployment. Existing PTQ methods mainly suppress channel-wise outliers by smoothing or orthogonal rotation. Geometry Transformers introduce a different challenge: heterogeneous token types share one activation quantization grid. A few special tokens may exert high leverage over global outputs and be updated by only a subset of blocks. Token-blind transforms, which apply the same transformation to every token, reduce overall quantization error but cannot remove differentiated degradation across token types. We propose QMapAnything, a structure-aware PTQ framework. We cast floating-point-preserving rotation and smoothing as a token-blind reparameterization group, and prove that they yield common-mode gains while leaving relative token SNR unchanged. We introduce a statistic $Γ$ that combines a special token's quantization disadvantage and output leverage to predict whether differentiated bias correction is needed beyond rotation. Experiments on geometric benchmarks show that W4A8 closely matches full-precision performance. Under W8A8, peak memory falls by $2.2$--$3.1\times$ versus FP32, with up to $3.1\times$ speedup on real hardware. These results provide a principled and efficient approach to deploying feed-forward 3D reconstruction models in resource-constrained edges.

On public benchmarks, RIVER achieves the highest success rates in both enzyme retrieval and enzyme annotation. In family-specific enzyme retrieval, RIVER outperforms the evaluated baselines on the selected datasets and metrics. In Escherichia coli enzyme retrieval tasks constructed from recent literature, RIVER achieves the best retrieval results with closed-source and open-source model configurations, substantially outperforming closed-source general-purpose and biology-specialized multi-agent systems, including Claude Code, Codex, and Phylo Max. These results suggest that evidence-guided multi-agent retrieval can prioritize biochemically supported enzyme--reaction relationships, providing a basis for subsequent experimental validation.

Wind farm power control can help the system maintain balance and avoid blackouts. In Europe, this can be achieved through implicit balancing, where a wind farm adjusts its power setpoint in anticipation of the imbalance price using information published by the Transmission System Operator. Thereby, the wind farm helps restore system balance while also reducing its own imbalance costs resulting from forecast errors. However, implicit balancing is a challenging problem since the settlement price is unknown in advance and can be impacted by the pursued control strategy. Reinforcement learning methods could help to solve this problem, but lack the interpretability needed to be deployed on real wind farms. Therefore, we propose a policy distillation framework using explainable boosting machines (EBMs), a variant of generalized additive models, to obtain a transparent control policy. To increase the value captured by the EBM model, we introduce a value-oriented perspective to policy distillation inspired by research in the field of energy forecasting. Furthermore, we suggest to include expert knowledge during model design, such that the control strategy is also justifiable to stakeholders. Using real data of the Belgian grid and an offshore wind farm, we find that our value-oriented and expert-based controller can reduce imbalance costs by 82%, while outperforming a price threshold controller as well as a policy distillation baseline using decision trees.

Tokamak experiments record diverse measurements, including waveforms, profiles, fields, and scalars, which are used to reconstruct the plasma, forecast its evolution, recover associated control signals, and estimate disruption risk. Most models are built separately for one task and a fixed set of inputs and outputs, and each relies on a prescribed subset of diagnostics rather than structuring the full multimodal observation set to extract global, cross-signal information about the discharge. These limitations motivate a reusable foundation model that adapts to each task's input-output schema and models heterogeneous target signals jointly, rather than retraining a separate model for every configuration. In this work, we extend the TokaMark benchmark with two groups of tasks for actuator action inversion and disruption prediction, yielding 20 MAST tasks in six groups with explicit schemas and evaluation protocols. Then, we introduce GenToka, a generative tokamak foundation model that jointly generates schema-selected targets through a shared conditional diffusion Transformer, with signal-specific codecs preserving native time series, profiles, fields, and scalars; foundation pretraining and task-specific adaptation transfer cross-signal structure across all six groups. Evaluation on MAST measurements shows that GenToka reduces macro-average normalized root mean squared error relative to TokaMind by 4.7% on Groups 1-4 and by 17.7% on Group 5, and improves macro-average window-level F1 on Group 6 by 8.4% at a common validation-selected threshold. GenToka provides a reusable, generative, and multimodal pathway for experimental tokamaks, linking plasma-state reconstruction, actuator inference, and disruption warning from shared measurements to support more integrated, data-driven fusion plasma prediction and control research.

As LLMs are increasingly asked questions about a user's personal experiences, retrieval must distinguish memories by time, place, person, and event. Current personal-memory pipelines rely on frozen dense embedding models to embed memories for retrieval, but we find that these models do not capture the four axes well. Swapping a memory's date or place barely shifts its embedding, and a trained projection on top of the frozen embedding does not recover the missing signal. The problem lives inside the model, where each axis is encoded by a distinct attention-head pattern. Guided by these findings, we propose Axis-LoRA, a per-axis adapter that places four LoRA branches inside the embedding model and combines them via a per-query gate. Across three standard embedding models evaluated on LongMemEval, ATM-Bench, and LoCoMo, Axis-LoRA improves 4-benchmark mean R@10 by $+1.28$ to $+1.56$~pp while training only a small fraction of the base parameters on a small axis-counterfactual training set. Code is available at https://github.com/dohoonkim-ai/axis-lora.

### Concertina: Embodying Manipulation and Cognition in One Diffusion Vision-Language Model
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07513) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07513) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07513) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07513)

Most vision-language-action (VLA) policies decouple manipulation from cognition by bolting an external action mechanism onto a vision-language model (VLM)--either quantizing actions into discrete tokens whose errors bear no stable correspondence to physical displacements, or delegating continuous trajectory generation to a separate action expert whose gradients compete with or are blocked from updating the VLM backbone. We argue that manipulation and cognition should instead be embodied in the same model, shifting from a decoupled VLA to an Embodied Vision-Language Model (eVLM). We introduce Concertina, an eVLM that places continuous trajectory representation, blockwise generation, and physical-coordinate supervision directly on the computation and parameter path of a shared diffusion VLM backbone. Each control timestep is encoded as one continuous latent action token in the backbone hidden space, and the shared Transformer reconstructs temporally ordered action blocks via the same corrupt-and-reconstruct operator used for multimodal pretraining. Active action projections account for only 0.21% of parameters in the 4.46B model with zero separate action-generator layers, compared with 16.5%-35.1% dedicated action-expert capacity in representative continuous VLAs. Using our proposed Action Interface Audit, we confirm that cross-time trajectory coupling emerges in the middle and late layers of the shared backbone rather than at the action interfaces. On standard benchmarks, Concertina achieves 98.15% average success on regular LIBERO, 85.9% on the 10,030-task LIBERO-Plus benchmark, and 4.142 average completed chain length on CALVIN ABC-D. Code: https://github.com/lose4578/Concertina Hugging Face: https://huggingface.co/collections/Traly/concertina

When a base language model is fine-tuned with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), how much of the training data domain leaks into the resulting artifact? This paper studies whether a simple classifier can recover the domain of a fine-tune given only the model artifact (weight deltas, internal activations on a fixed probe set, or generated text) without ever observing the training data. Using one base model (Qwen3.5-0.8B), 36 LoRA fine-tunes are produced over 18 public datasets in five domains (medical, code, mathematics, non-English text, narrative prose), and three feature families are extracted at decreasing levels of access: white-box weights, gray-box activations, and black-box outputs. A leave-one-dataset-out (LODO) protocol, frozen before any result was seen, tests whether the domain of a fine-tune trained on a previously unseen dataset can be identified. The domain is recoverable well above chance (20%) from gray-box activations (61%) and black-box outputs (64%); white-box weight statistics reach 42%. Contrary to the pre-registered hypothesis that signal decays monotonically from weights to activations to outputs, activations and outputs were more informative than the weight-delta statistics. The black-box result holds unchanged (64%) when only generated text is used, without token probabilities. Domain accuracy is also stable when a third run per dataset is trained on an independently drawn data subsample, and black-box predictions are unaffected by the LoRA rank (8-64). All code and per-run feature vectors are released.

### GraspBench: Execution-Aware Cross-Robot Evaluation of 6-DoF Grasp Generation in MuJoCo
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07651) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07651) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07651) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07651)

Modern 6-DoF grasp generators are often evaluated using grasp scores, dataset-level metrics, or single-robot demonstrations, yet proposal quality alone does not determine whether a grasp is executable by a particular robot. We present GraspBench, a controlled MuJoCo protocol for evaluating complete grasp-generation pipelines after robot-specific inverse-kinematics filtering, motion planning, and fixed approach-close-lift execution. GraspBench evaluates six configured pipelines across four robot embodiments, five diagnostic objects, and a structured tabletop pose grid, yielding 540 scenarios per method and 3,240 method-scenario evaluations in total. Missing outputs are conservatively counted as failures, and success follows the binary lift criterion produced by each method's execution runner. GraspGen achieves the highest overall success rate (73.1%), followed by EconomicGrasp (63.5%), while robot- and object-conditioned analyses reveal substantial variation across embodiments and object geometries. GPG produces the largest native candidate sets but achieves 44.3% success, indicating that proposal volume alone does not guarantee robot-executable grasps. Cluster-aware paired intervals support the principal method differences while not estimating repeated-execution uncertainty. The results characterize complete configured pipelines on fixed scenarios rather than intrinsic model quality or stochastic repeatability, showing that grasping performance depends jointly on the generator, robot embodiment, scene, and execution protocol.

### Beneath the Surface: What We Overlook When We Talk About Agent Evolution
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07624) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07624) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07624) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07624)

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

Agent harness evolution enables recursive self-improvement (RSI) without updating model weights. We examine whether higher benchmark scores yield reliable, efficient, and transferable improvement. We evaluate four methods across harnesses, general and coding tasks. Repeated evaluations, controlled agent expansion, component interventions, and admission replays reveal recurring patterns. All sixteen evolved agents gain previously uncovered tasks while losing previously covered ones. In one general-task case, reliability rises by 5.2 points despite losing ten previously covered tasks. Most reliability gains also increase deployment cost. Specific changes usually outperform generic reminders, but their benefits can change when combined. The highest-scoring agent does not yield the largest mean improvement in any of our four pools, and stricter admission can discard useful alternatives. Transfer selectively preserves gains: coverage usually falls on the general-task target across both harness types and paradigms, but rises on the coding target with the outer-layer-only harness. These findings motivate preservation-aware evaluation, tested decision rules, and explicit applicability conditions. As a constructive follow-up, EvoAudit turns these diagnostics into revisions of select, analyze, mutate, and gate, achieving the best performance.

### Benchmarks as Software: Terminal-Bench and the Case for Continuous Benchmarks
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08417) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08417) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08417) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.08417)

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

Coding agents are among the most economically consequential deployments of LLMs to date, making reliable evaluation increasingly important. Yet even benchmarks that are carefully validated at release can become stale. As models improve, they can discover valid solutions that existing verifiers reject, while changes to evaluation infrastructure can invalidate assumptions embedded in tasks and tests. We call the resulting accumulation of defects benchmark maintenance debt: a growing gap between benchmark scores and the capabilities they are intended to measure. We introduce a continuous-validation workflow for agentic benchmarks that combines community reports, task artifacts, agent traces, automated oracle checks, and human-gated repairs. We apply this workflow to Terminal-Bench 2.0, a benchmark that underwent extensive human and LM-assisted review and red-teaming before release. Our audit identifies issues in roughly 30% of the benchmark, and we release the resulting repairs as Terminal-Bench 2.1. These repairs change evaluation results: accuracy increased by over 10 percentage points and leaderboard positions move by up to three ranks, even as the overall ranking remains highly correlated (Spearman rho = 0.958). We evaluate how resource allocation shapes benchmark conclusions. We show that fixed wall-clock can materially change agent performance: For example, extending the time limit closes a 20pp harness gap on GPT 5.4 and shrinks a 30 pp gap on GPT 5.4 mini to 5 pp. Benchmark maintenance is therefore not only about repairing task artifacts: resource budgets are part of the measurement specification and must be validated alongside the tasks themselves. More broadly, benchmark maintenance debt is not just accumulated implementation bugs: our results show that as models improve, both evaluation assumptions and resource settings may need to be revalidated.

### CORE: Coarse-to-Fine Search with Context Management for Deep Research
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07859) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07859) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07859) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07859)

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

Deep research agents answer complex questions through repeated search. They rely on a harness that runs their tool calls and returns evidence to their context. This creates a trade-off: Search works best over long passages, which give enough context for reliable semantic matching. But the returned evidence should be short, because every observation stays in the agent's context for the rest of the trajectory. We introduce CORE, a training-free harness that combines coarse-to-fine search with context management. CORE retrieves passages with a dense retriever. It then reuses the token-level similarities from a late-interaction reranker to return only the most relevant snippet from each passage. Context management lets the agent expand the context around sources it has already retrieved and suppresses repeated snippets. In controlled closed-search comparisons with the Tongyi DeepResearch and OpenResearcher harnesses, CORE achieves state-of-the-art answer accuracy and evidence recall across three agent models and three benchmarks (BrowseComp-Plus, BrowseComp-Wikipedia and BrowseComp-Plus-CM), on corpora containing up to 553M documents.

### A Behavioural Framework for Predicting and Understanding Loss of Control in Frontier Artificial Intelligence Systems
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07953v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07953v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07953v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07953v1)

2026/11/05 12:49 GTM

Frontier artificial intelligence (AI) systems are increasingly operating over longer horizons with diminishing human oversight, raising the risk of loss of control: systems acting beyond effective human control with potentially irreversible consequences. Because such failures may become observable only after containment is difficult, they must be assessed prospectively. We introduce a behavioural framework that decomposes loss-of-control risk into three factors: misaligned motive, harm-enabling capability, and monitoring evasion. We operationalise these factors through 13 measurable aspects and 29 evaluation tasks, whose scores are combined into a single loss-of-control (LoC) score. Across 13 frontier models, the LoC score shows strong prospective validity, correlating with failure rates in contained long-horizon risk scenarios (Pearson r = 0.84). Risk varies systematically across models and cannot be explained by capability alone. We further show that the framework can guide targeted mitigations: fine-tuning and monitoring directed at diagnosed vulnerabilities reduce failure rates while largely preserving general performance. These results provide a practical basis for predicting, understanding, and mitigating loss-of-control risk before it materialises.

### RouteAlign: Aligning Task Requirements with Model Profiles for LLM Service Selection
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08162v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08162v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08162v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.08162v1)

2026/11/05 15:21 GTM

LLM service selection requires performance evidence that reflects the demands of the current task. Aggregate scores obscure differences in capability and difficulty, while semantically related examples can involve different combinations of skills. We present RouteAlign, which uses task requirements to structure both the measurement of model performance and the selection of services. Task requirements describe capability demand and difficulty, enabling alignment with corresponding model performance measurements. Historical requirements determine how outcomes contribute to global profiles and local capability estimates; incoming requirements determine how those measurements inform the current decision. A shared scorer integrates the evidence, and a generation-cost preference controls selection among eligible services. Evaluation across diverse tasks and heterogeneous LLM services shows that RouteAlign extends the empirical quality-cost frontier toward higher task quality. Comparisons with open Jev-style decision backends show consistent quality gains, with lower generation cost than their strongest tested configuration. Ablations support the contribution of local performance evidence, while catalog-update experiments demonstrate effective reuse of the fitted scorer with refreshed model measurements.

### Randomized Parallel Complexity of Weakly Convex Optimization
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07675) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07675) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07675) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07675)

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

We study the minimum number of query rounds needed to find approximate stationary points of nonsmooth weakly convex functions, measured by the gradient of the Moreau envelope. Each round permits multiple simultaneous queries. In the stated parameter regime, the standard subgradient rate is optimal in sufficiently high dimensions, even for randomized algorithms making polynomially many parallel queries per round. We then show how the order structure of the real line enables faster search: a deterministic algorithm using only function values achieves logarithmic round complexity, with a further reduction through parallel queries. Matching lower bounds hold even when each query returns the entire subdifferential. Together, these results clarify the role of dimension in determining the benefits of parallel queries for weakly convex optimization.

### Goal-Branching LaCAM: Temporary Goals as Search Branches in Dense Multi-Agent Pathfinding
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07528) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07528) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07528) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07528)

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

LaCAM is a search-based solver for multi-agent pathfinding (MAPF) that runs a depth-first search over joint configurations, using PIBT to generate successors and lazily adding single-agent constraints to explore alternatives. Although LaCAM is complete, it can struggle on dense, corridor-heavy instances because extracting blocking agents from deadlocks requires coordinated moves of several agents, often away from their goals, which PIBT's greedy one-step successors rarely propose and which LaCAM can only reach by enumerating constraints one agent at a time. This paper proposes Goal-Branching, where blocking agents are given temporary goals, each tried as an additional branch of LaCAM's search. We examine random and corridor-aware rules for choosing these temporary goals and show that completeness is preserved for any such rule. On dense instances (60-90% of free cells occupied) of three maze and room maps, Goal-Branching solves 89% of instances against 44% for LaCAM*, and, on instances both solve, it finds a first solution 10-14x faster on maze-32-32-2 and room-32-32-4, and nearly 200x faster in median time at 75% density on room-32-32-4. Solution cost increases by 15-22% on instances both solve, while on open maps and the full MovingAI benchmark its solutions cost no more than those of LaCAM* and it is at most 3% slower.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07520) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07520) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07520) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07520)

Uncertainty in resource reopening times complicates train holding and rerouting decisions when resource closures coincide with arrival delays and processing-time extensions. We develop a rolling-horizon distributionally robust model that updates predictions of the remaining closure duration as observations of disruption resolution become available. The model jointly optimizes train routes, sequences, and timings. It minimizes the worst-case expected total completion delay over an ambiguity set of scenario probabilities. The solution algorithm, GNN-LBBD, uses logic-based Benders decomposition to coordinate routing and resource scheduling. Conditional cuts are constructed from resource-use patterns, and a graph neural network guides the order in which resource relaxation subproblems are solved. Across 28 test instances for a five-track station, GNN-LBBD achieved a mean total completion delay of 61.13 train-min. This was 16.63\% lower than tabu search and 20.06\% lower than variable neighborhood search, with a mean total computation time close to that of tabu search. Information comparisons on six representative instances showed that using observations of disruption resolution reduced mean total completion delay by approximately 21.7\%. Scenario-based and conditional-mean predictions produced similar overall results under the same information conditions. The benefits of information were more pronounced in some instances with alternative routes. This suggests a relationship between the use of observations, the station resource structure, and the scope for schedule adjustments.

### Same Behavior, Different Mechanism: What Finite Experiments Can Certify About Integrated Information
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08469) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08469) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08469) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.08469)

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

Interpretability, AI auditing and assessments of machine consciousness increasingly rest on mechanism-level quantities: functionals of a system's causal transition law that describe how it computes, not what it outputs. Experiments see that law only through finite, partial and noisy interventions. We ask when such a quantity is identifiable from an experiment's access, continuous in the mechanism and certifiable from finite data, and answer exactly for a deliberately demanding case - the integrated information $\varphi$ of integrated information theory - in stochastic networks of four and five units with known ground truth. Behavior can be silent: a recurrent and a feed-forward network with identical input-output laws at their response delay carry 0.28 and 0 bits, yet internal recordings separate them within 4-53 trials. Partial access can be just as blind: in our examples, units left out of a one-step recording impose precision floors of 0.30-1.02 bits that no sample size lowers, and in all-but-self parity networks, recordings of any length see several hidden units only through the product of their fidelities - a symmetry that one intervention broke in exact checks. The measure has sharp edges: because $\varphi$ selects a partition, and the cause and effect it specifies, by one criterion and reports another, infinitesimal changes of mechanism that cross an edge change it by 25-100% at 99% of the edges we found in random networks, and about 1% of surrogate models that fit interventional data almost perfectly misreport it by more than 20%. One-sided claims survive: the same data certify a lower bound on both sides of an edge, and we prove $\varphi>1.004$ bits throughout a 64-dimensional region of mechanisms and certify it from about $10^7$ controlled transitions with false-certificate probability at most $α$. Measurability is thus a design question with computable answers.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07542) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07542) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07542) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07542)

In large language model (LLM) serving systems, the key-value (KV) cache grows linearly with sequence length and concurrency, so long-context inference easily hits the high-bandwidth memory (HBM) capacity wall and results in low concurrency. Offloading the KV cache to host dynamic random-access memory (DRAM) frees HBM space and raises concurrency, but the additional cache management and host-to-device (H2D) load overhead inflates inference latency, higher concurrency fails to translate into effective throughput. We present FlashOffloading, a host-DRAM-centric inference architecture that fully offloads the KV cache in both the prefill and decode phases. FlashOffloading builds rolling HBM cache for prefill, a hot cache for decode, a reverse sparse index with zero extra miss-lookup overhead, and a compute--load overlapped kernel, optimizing KV offloading latency across the entire pipeline and delivering throughput gains under a time-per-output-token (TPOT) service-level objective (SLO). In experiments deploying GLM-5.2 with multi-token prediction (MTP3) speculative decoding on an Ascend910C prefill--decode (PD)-disaggregated platform, FlashOffloading improves prefill throughput by up to 1.38\(\times\) and decode throughput by up to 1.91\(\times\) under a 30 ms TPOT constraint. FlashOffloading is integrated into an open-source serving framework: https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm-ascend/tree/releases/v0.30.0.

### SeVerus: Context-Aware, Gamified Benchmarking for Rust Verification
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07194) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.07194) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07194) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.07194)

2026/11/06 12:33 GTM

Coding agents achieve high success rates on existing benchmarks for Verus, a verifier for Rust programs. These benchmarks typically provide the auxiliary lemmas needed to complete a proof. In practice, however, such lemmas must be discovered and proved. To capture this challenge, we present SEVERUS, a context-aware technique to generate Verus benchmarks that organizes verification tasks into quests, each divided into levelsin a gamified manner. Using the original proof dependencies in verified projects, SEVERUS constructs one quest per verification target and progressively removes proof context to create individual levels. These levels range from the easiest, requiring completing only a single missing lemma, to the hardest, requiring proving the target without any supporting lemmas. An independent checker rejects changes to executable code, weakened specifications, and unsound proof constructs. A noisy binary search estimates the highest level that an agent can solve reliably. Applied to six open-source Verus projects, SEVERUS generates 819 quests comprising 10,095 levels. Experiments with Codex using GPT-6 Sol and GPT-6 Luna, and with Claude Code using Sonnet 4.6, show that success decreases and cost increases as proof context is withdrawn. The strongest agent solves the final level of 52 of 60 sampled quests, yet can only reliably solve 35% of their levels, with failures concentrated in longer quests. Successful GPT-6 Sol proofs rarely reproduce the withheld lemmas, instead, using alternative proof decompositions. By systematically varying proof context, SEVERUS provides a foundation for constructing verification benchmarks that can continue to challenge increasingly capable coding agents.
